\subsection{Systems of \texorpdfstring{$p$}{p}-generators and \texorpdfstring{$p$}{p}-bases}
\label{subsection:0.21.1}

\begin{env}[21.1.1]
\label{0.21.1.1}
Given a number $p$ which is either $0$, or a prime number, we say that a ring $A$ is \emph{of characteristic $p$} if there exists a ring homomorphism $P\to A$, where $P$ is the \emph{prime field} of characteristic $p$; we note that this homomorphism is then unique, the composite $\bb{Z}\to P\to A$ being the unique homomorphism of $\bb{Z}$ into $A$.
If $A\neq 0$, this amounts to saying that $A$ \emph{contains a field of characteristic $p$}, the image of $P$ being necessarily a field isomorphic to $P$ (and moreover the only subfield of $A$ isomorphic to $P$).
\end{env}

\begin{env}[21.1.2]
\label{0.21.1.2}
If $p>0$, to say that $A$ is of characteristic $p$ is equivalent to saying that, in $A$, $p.1=0$, or equivalently $pA=0$.
If $p=0$, to say that $A$ is of characteristic $p$ is equivalent to saying that for every integer $n\neq 0$, $n.1$ is \emph{invertible} in $A$.
If $A\neq 0$, there can exist only one $p$ (prime or $0$) such that $A$ is of characteristic $p$; this follows from what precedes and from the identity of Bezout $ap+bq=1$ for two distinct primes $p$, $q$.
On the other hand, the ring reduced to $0$ is of characteristic $p$ for \emph{every} $p$.
\end{env}

\begin{env}[21.1.3]
\label{0.21.1.3}
If $A$ is of characteristic $p$, then so is every \emph{algebra} over $A$.
In particular, for every prime ideal $\mathfrak{p}$ of $A$, the \emph{residue field} of $A$ at $\mathfrak{p}$ is of characteristic $p$.
Conversely, if $p=0$ and if for every \emph{maximal} ideal $\mathfrak{m}$ of $A$, the residue field of $A$ at $\mathfrak{m}$ is of characteristic $0$, then so is $A$, since for every integer $n\neq 0$, $n.1$ is then invertible in all the $A_\mathfrak{m}$, hence also in $A$.
On the other hand, if $p>0$, a local ring can have its residue field of characteristic $p$ without being itself of characteristic $p$, as shown by the example of the \emph{prime ring} (integral) $\bb{Z}_{p\bb{Z}}$ \sref{0.19.8.3} or of the local Artinian ring $\bb{Z}/p^n\bb{Z}$ for $n\geq 2$, which do not contain a field.

We note finally that for a ring (even reduced), the residue fields at its prime ideals can have different characteristics, as shown by the example of $\bb{Z}$.
\end{env}

\begin{env}[21.1.4]
\label{0.21.1.4}
In all the rest of this section, we fix a \emph{prime number $p$} and all rings will be assumed \emph{of characteristic $p$}, unless express mention to the contrary.
For such a ring $A$, the map $x\leadsto x^p$ is an \emph{endomorphism} of $A$, which we denote $\mathrm{F}_A$; if $A$ is \emph{reduced}, $\mathrm{F}_A$ is \emph{injective}.
We set $A^p=\mathrm{F}_A(A)$ (subring of $A$ formed by the $x^p$ for $x\in A$); one can naturally consider $A$ as an $A^p$-algebra.

One can also consider $A$ as an $A$-\emph{algebra by means of the homomorphism} $\mathrm{F}_A:x\leadsto x^p$ of $A$ into $A$; in other words, this is the $A$-algebra $A_{[\mathrm{F}_A]}$, for which the product $\lambda.x$ of $x\in A$ by a scalar $\lambda\in A$ is the product $\lambda^p x$ in the ring $A$; we denote this $A$-algebra $A^{(p)}$.
It is clear that for every ring homomorphism $u:A\to B$ the pair $(u,u)$ is a \emph{di-homomorphism} of algebras $A^{(p)}\to B^{(p)}$.
For every $A$-module $E$, we set $E^{(p)}=E\otimes_A A^{(p)}$ where $A^{(p)}$ is considered as an $(A,A)$-bimodule, the structure of $A$-module on the left being that which one has just defined, and the structure of $A$-module on the right being defined by the multiplication in $A$; $E^{(p)}$ is equipped with the structure of $A$-module coming from the structure of $A$-module on the right of $A^{(p)}$, so that for $x\in E$, $\alpha$, $\beta$ in $A$, one has
\[
  \alpha(x\otimes\beta)=x\otimes(\alpha\beta) \quad\text{and}\quad (\alpha x)\otimes\beta=\alpha^p(x\otimes\beta)=x\otimes\alpha^p\beta.
\]
----- French corrections on page 0\textsubscript{IV-1} 153 -----
[id 10287]
OLD: nécessairement continu.
NEW: nécessairement \emph{continu}.
FRENCH CONTEXT (corrected): ible à gauche.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
En effet, les topologies de
$\widehat{\Omega}^1_{B/A}\widehat\otimes_B C$ et de
$\widehat{\Omega}^1_{C/A}$ sont alors déduites de celle de $C$
\sref{0.20.4.5-fr}, et tout $C$-homomorphisme de l'un dans l'autre est
nécessairement \emph{continu}.
\end{proof}

Setting $x^{(p)}=x\otimes 1$, one therefore has $(\alpha x)^{(p)}=\alpha^p x^{(p)}$.
When $\mathrm{F}_A$ is \emph{injective}, one can identify $A^{(p)}$ with the ring $A$ considered as an algebra over its \emph{subring} $A^p$.
\end{env}

\begin{proposition}[21.1.5]
\label{0.21.1.5}
Let $A$, $B$ be two rings, $u:A\to B$ a homomorphism.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\rm(i)] Every $A$-derivation of $B$ in a $B$-module $L$ is zero in $A[B^p]$ \textup{(}hence is an $A[B^p]$-derivation\textup{)}.
  \item[\rm\textup{(ii)}] For every sub-$A$-algebra $A'$ of $A[B^p]$, if $j:A'\to B$ is the canonical injection, the canonical homomorphism
\[
  j_{B/A'/A}:\Omega^1_{B/A}\to\Omega^1_{B/A'}
\]
is bijective.
  \item[\rm\textup{(iii)}] Suppose that there exists an integer $s\geq 0$ such that $u(A)\supset B^{p^s}$.
  Then, in the ring $B\otimes_A B$, $\mathfrak{J}_{B/A}$ is a nilideal.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(i) By induction from \sref{0.20.1.1.1}, one deduces, for every $A$-derivation $D:B\to L$, that $D(x^k)=kx^{k-1}D(x)$, whence in particular $D(x^p)=0$.

(ii) In view of \sref{0.20.4.8}, the assertion (ii) is only a translation of (i), this last being written $\Der_A(B,L)=\Der_{A[B^p]}(B,L)$ for every $B$-module $L$.

(iii) For every $x\in B$, one has $(x\otimes 1\setmin1\otimes x)^{p^s}=x^{p^s}\otimes 1\setmin1\otimes x^{p^s}=x^{p^s}(1\otimes 1\setmin1\otimes 1)=0$, since $x^{p^s}\in u(A)$.
The conclusion follows from the fact that the elements $1\otimes x\setmin x\otimes 1$ generate $\mathfrak{J}_{B/A}$ \sref{0.20.4.4}.
\end{proof}

It follows immediately from \sref{0.21.1.5} that one has, for every pair of ring homomorphisms $A\to B\to C$,
\begin{equation}
\tag{21.1.5.1}
  \label{0.21.1.5.1}
  \Upsilon_{C/B/A}=\Upsilon_{C/B/A'}
\end{equation}
for every sub-$A$-algebra $A'$ of $A[B^p]$.

On the other hand, \sref{0.21.1.5} also shows in particular that for the modules of ``absolute'' differentials
\begin{equation}
\tag{21.1.5.2}
  \label{0.21.1.5.2}
  \Omega^1_A=\Omega^1_{A/A^p}
\end{equation}

\begin{corollary}[21.1.6]
\label{0.21.1.6}
Suppose that $B$ is an $A$-algebra of finite type and that there exists an integer $s\geq 0$ such that $u(A)\supset B^{p^s}$.
If $\Omega^1_{B/A}=0$, $u$ is surjective.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}

By \sref{0.21.1.5}, (iii), $\mathfrak{J}_{B/A}$ is a nilideal; moreover, by virtue of \sref{0.20.4.4} and the hypothesis, $\mathfrak{J}_{B/A}$ is an ideal of finite type, hence it is \emph{nilpotent}; as the relation $\Omega^1_{B/A}=0$ signifies that $\mathfrak{J}_{B/A}=\mathfrak{J}^2_{B/A}$, one concludes that $\mathfrak{J}_{B/A}=0$, or equivalently that $\pi:B\otimes_A B\to B$ is \emph{bijective}.
On the other hand, every element of $B$ having its $p^s$-th power in $u(A)$, $B$ is \emph{integral} over $A$, and being of finite type, it is a \emph{finite} $A$-algebra.
One is thus reduced to proving the following lemma (where one \emph{does not assume} that the rings considered are of characteristic $p$):
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[21.1.6.1]
\label{0.21.1.6.1}
Let $R$ be a ring, $S$ a finite $R$-algebra; if the canonical homomorphism $\pi:S\otimes_R S\to S$ is bijective, then the structural homomorphism $u:R\to S$ is surjective.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
It suffices to show that for every maximal ideal $\mathfrak{m}$ of $R$, if one sets $T=R\setmin\mathfrak{m}$, the homomorphism $u_\mathfrak{m}:R_\mathfrak{m}\to T^{-1}S$ is surjective (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~II, \S\,3, n$^\circ$\,3, th.~1); now the hypothesis implies that the homomorphism $(T^{-1}S)\otimes_{R_\mathfrak{m}}(T^{-1}S)\to T^{-1}S$ is bijective \sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.1.3.4}, and as $T^{-1}S$ is a finite $R_\mathfrak{m}$-algebra, one sees that one can reduce to the case where $R$ is a \emph{local} ring.
Denoting again by $\mathfrak{m}$ its maximal ideal, it suffices to prove that $u\otimes 1:R/\mathfrak{m}\to S/\mathfrak{m}S$ is surjective, by virtue of the lemma of Nakayama ($S$ being an $R$-module of finite type); as the canonical homomorphism $(S/\mathfrak{m}S)\otimes_{R/\mathfrak{m}}(S/\mathfrak{m}S)\to S/\mathfrak{m}S$ is bijective and $S/\mathfrak{m}S$ is a finite $(R/\mathfrak{m})$-algebra, one is finally reduced to the case where $R$ is a \emph{field}; but then the ranks of $S\otimes_R S$ and of $S$ over $R$ must be equal, which is possible only if $S$ is of rank $0$ or $1$, hence $u:R\to S$ is surjective.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[21.1.7]
\label{0.21.1.7}
Let $A$ be a ring, $B$ an $A$-algebra, $(x_\alpha)_{\alpha\in I}$ a family of elements of $B$.
Consider the following properties:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\rm a)] $B=A[B^p,(x_\alpha)]$, or equivalently the $A[B^p]$-algebra $B$ is generated by the family $(x_\alpha)_{\alpha\in I}$.
  \item[\rm b)] The $A[B^p]$-module $B$ is generated by the monomials $\prod_\alpha x_\alpha^{n(\alpha)}$, where $(n(\alpha))_{\alpha\in I}$ is a family of integers with finite support such that $0\leq n(\alpha)<p$ for all $\alpha\in I$.
  \item[\rm c)] The $B$-module $\Omega^1_{B/A}$ is generated by the $d_{B/A}(x_\alpha)$ $(\alpha\in I)$.
\end{enumerate}
Then the properties \textup{a)} and \textup{b)} are equivalent and imply \textup{c)}; if in addition $B$ is an $A[B^p]$-algebra of finite type, \textup{c)} is equivalent to \textup{a)} and \textup{b)}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
It is clear that b) implies a), and conversely a) implies b), since every monomial $\prod_\alpha x_\alpha^{m(\alpha)}$, where the $m(\alpha)$ are integers $\geq 0$ (forming a family with finite support), can be written $(\prod_\alpha x_\alpha^{q(\alpha)})^p\prod_\alpha x_\alpha^{r(\alpha)}$, by dividing each $m(\alpha)$ by $p$, which gives $m(\alpha)=p.q(\alpha)+r(\alpha)$ with $0\leq r(\alpha)<p$.
The fact that a) implies c) follows from \sref{0.21.1.5}, (ii) and \sref{0.20.4.7}.

Suppose conversely c) satisfied and that $B$ is an $A[B^p]$-algebra of finite type; let $B'$ be the sub-$A[B^p]$-algebra of $B$ generated by the $x_\alpha$; in the exact sequence \sref{0.20.5.7.1}
\[
  \Omega^1_{B'/A[B^p]}\otimes_{B'}B\to\Omega^1_{B/A[B^p]}\to\Omega^1_{B/B'}\to 0
\]
the hypothesis implies that the left arrow is surjective (taking into account \sref{0.21.1.5}, (ii)); one therefore has $\Omega^1_{B/B'}=0$, and as $B^p\subset B'\subset B$ and $B$ is a $B'$-algebra of finite type, one necessarily has $B'=B$ by \sref{0.21.1.6}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[21.1.8]
\label{0.21.1.8}
When $B$ is a \emph{field}, we will demonstrate the equivalence of properties a), b) and c) without hypothesis of finiteness \sref{0.21.4.5}.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[21.1.9]
\label{0.21.1.9}
Let $A$ be a ring (of characteristic $p$), $B$ an $A$-algebra.
One says that a family $(x_\alpha)_{\alpha\in I}$ of elements of $B$ is \emph{$p$-free over $A$} (resp.\ a \emph{system of $p$-generators} of $B$ over $A$, \textup{resp.}\ a \emph{$p$-base} of $B$ over $A$) if the family of monomials $\prod_\alpha x_\alpha^{n(\alpha)}$ $(0\leq n(\alpha)<p$, $(n(\alpha))_{\alpha\in I}$ with finite support) is a free family (resp.\ a system of generators, resp.\ a base) in the $A[B^p]$-module $B$.
\end{definition}

One has corresponding definitions for a \emph{subset} $M$ of elements of $B$, by considering the family defined by the canonical injection $M\to B$.
When one takes for $A$ the \emph{prime field} $\bb{F}_p$ (in which case $A[B^p]=B^p$), one omits the mention of $A$ in the preceding definition (or also says that a family is ``absolutely'' $p$-free, resp.\ an ``absolute'' system of $p$-generators, resp.\ an ``absolute'' $p$-base).
